On the Finite Variable-Occurrence Fragment of the Calculus of Relations with Bounded Dot-Dagger Alternation
Yoshiki Nakamura

Abstract
We introduce the k k -variable-occurrence fragment , which is the set of terms having at most k k occurrences of variables. We give a sufficient condition for the decidability of the equational theory of the k k -variable-occurrence fragment using the finiteness of a monoid. As a case study, we prove that for Tarski’s calculus of relations with bounded dot-dagger alternation (an analogy of quantifier alternation in first-order logic), the equational theory of the k k -variable-occurrence fragment is decidable for each k k .



Tokyo Institute of Technology, Japannakamura.yoshiki.ny@gmail.comhttps://orcid.org/0000-0003-4106-0408

We would like to thank the anonymous reviewers for their useful comments.

1
Introduction

Since
the satisfiability problem of first-order logic
is undecidable
[
9
,
32
]
in general,
(un-)decidable classes of first-order logic are widely studied
[
8
]
;
for example, the undecidability holds even for the
Kahr–Moore–Wang (KMW) class
1
1

1

Recall the notation for
prefix-vocabulary classes

[
8
, Def. 1.3.1]
.
E.g.,
[\forall\exists\forall,(0,\omega)]
denotes the set of prenex sentences
\varphi
of first-order logic without equality, function symbols, nor constants such that
the quantifier prenex of
\varphi
is
\forall\exists\forall
;

\varphi
has
\omega
(countably infinitely many) binary relation symbols and
\varphi
does not have 1- nor
i
-ary relation symbols for
i\geq 3
.

[\forall\exists\forall,(0,\omega)]

[
16
]
,
but it is decidable for the
Bernays–Schönfinkel–Ramsey (BSR) class

[\exists^{*}\forall^{*},\mathrm{all}]_{=}

[
4
,
29
]
.
The calculus of relations (CoR)
[
30
]
, revived by Tarski, is an algebraic system on binary relations; its expressive power is equivalent to that of the three-variable fragment of first-order logic with equality
[
30
,
31
]
, w.r.t. binary relations.
The equational theory of CoR is undecidable
[
30
,
31
]
2
2

2

In
[
31
]
, the undecidability of the equational theory is shown for more general classes of relation algebras.
in general, which follows from the undecidability of the KMW class,
but, for example, it is decidable for the
(existential) positive fragment

[
2
,
28
]
and the
existential fragment

[
26
]
of CoR, which follows from the decidability of the BSR class.
On the undecidability of CoR, the undecidability holds even for the
1
-variable fragment
[
19
]
and even for the
1
-variable fragment only with union, composition, and complement
[
22
]
, where the
k
-variable fragment
denotes the set of terms having at most
k
variables.

Then, from the undecidability result for the
1
-variable fragment of CoR
[
19
,
22
]
above,
the following natural question arises—Is it decidable for the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment of CoR?
Here, the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment denotes the set of terms having at most
k
occurrences of variables.
For example, when
a,b
are variables and
\mathsf{I}
is a constant,
the term
(a\cdot b)\cdot(\mathsf{I}\cdot(a\cdot b))
has
4
occurrences of variables
and
1
occurrence of constants;
thus, this term is in the
4
-variable-occurrence fragment (cf. the term is in the
2
-variable fragment since the variables
a,b
occur).
While one may seem that this restriction immediately implies the decidability, the equational theory of the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment on some (single) algebra is undecidable in general even when
k=0
(
Remark

2
).

Our contribution is to prove that the equational theory of the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment is decidable for CoR
with bounded dot-dagger alternation
,
where the dot-dagger alternation
[
23
,
24
]
is an analogy of the quantifier alternation in first-order logic.
Note that the equational theory of the
k
-variable fragment is undecidable in general for CoR
[
19
,
22
]
(even with bounded dot-dagger alternation (
Proposition

23
)).
Our strategy is to prove that the number of terms in the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment is finite up to the semantic equivalence relation.
To this end, (1) we decompose terms as much as possible; and then (2) we show that each decomposed part is finite up to the semantic equivalence relation by collecting valid equations.
By the preprocessing of
1
, one can see that for
1
, essentially, it suffices to prove the finiteness of some monoid (using the method of
Section

2
).
Its finiteness is not clear, as it is undecidable whether a (finitely presented) monoid is finite in general;
but, fortunately, we can prove the finiteness (
Theorem

24
) by finding valid equations (
Figure

1
).

The rest of this paper is structured as follows.

Section

2
introduces the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment for general algebras and gives a framework to prove the decidability from the finiteness of a monoid.

Section

3
recalls the syntax and semantics of CoR and the dot-dagger alternation hierarchy.
In
Section

4
, based on
Section

2
, we prove that the equational theory of CoR with bounded dot-dagger alternation is decidable.

Section

5
concludes this paper.

We write
{\rm Nature}
for the set of all non-negative integers.
For
l,r\in{\rm Nature}
, we write
\immediate\immediate[l,r\immediate\immediate]
for the set
\{i\in{\rm Nature}\mid l\leq i\leq r\}
.
For a set
A
, we write
\mathop{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\#}}A
for the cardinality of
A
and
\mathop{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\wp}}(A)
for the power set of
A
.
For a set
A
and an equivalence relation
\sim
on
A
, we write
A\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}/}{\sim}
for the quotient set of
A
by
\sim
and
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}[}a{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}]}_{\sim}
for the equivalence class of an element
a\in A
on
\sim
.

2
On the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment

We fix
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Sigma}
as a non-empty
finite
set of variables.
We fix
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}S}
as a
finite
algebraic signature;
S
is a map from a finite domain (of functions) to
{\rm Nature}
.
For each
\langle f,n\rangle\in S
, we write
f^{(n)}
; it is the function symbol
f
with arity
n
.
We also let
S^{(n)}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{f^{(m)}\in S\mid m=n\}
.
The set
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}
of
S
-terms
over
\Sigma
is defined as the minimal set closed under the following two rules:

a\in\Sigma\Longrightarrow a\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}
; (
f^{(n)}\in S
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbf{T}
)
\Longrightarrow

f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}
.

An
S
-algebra

A
is a tuple
\langle\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}|}A{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}|},\{f^{A}\}_{f^{(n)}\in S}\rangle
, where
|A|
is a non-empty finite set and
f^{A}\colon|A|^{n}\to|A|
is an
n
-ary map for each
f^{(n)}\in S
.
A
valuation

\mathfrak{v}\colon\Sigma\to|A|
on an
S
-algebra
A
is a map;
we write
\hat{\mathfrak{v}}\colon\mathbf{T}\to|A|
for the unique homomorphism extending
\mathfrak{v}
.
For a class
\mathcal{C}
of
S
-algebras,
the equivalence relation
\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\sim}_{\mathcal{C}}}
on
\mathbf{T}
is defined by:

t\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\sim}_{\mathcal{C}}}s\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\iff$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\mbox{$\hat{\mathfrak{v}}(t)=\hat{\mathfrak{v}}(s)$ for all valuations $\mathfrak{v}$ on all algebras in $\mathcal{C}$}
.
For a set
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}
, the
equational theory of
T
over
\mathcal{C}
is the set
\{\langle t,s\rangle\in T^{2}\mid t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}s\}
.

Definition 1

(
k
-variable-occurrence fragment)
.

For an
S
-term
t
, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}}(t)
be the number of occurrences of variables in
t
:

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}}(t)\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\begin{cases}1&(t\in\Sigma)\\
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(t_{i})&(t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}))\end{cases}
.
For each set
T
of
S
-terms,
the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment

T_{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}}
is the set
\{t\in T\mid\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(t)\leq k\}
.
(Similarly, let
T_{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{t\in T\mid\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(t)=k\}
.)
Clearly,
T=\bigcup_{k\in{\rm Nature}}T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
.

Remark 2
.

The equational theory of the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment is undecidable in general, even when
k=0
.
It follows from the reduction from the word problem for monoids.
Let
M=\langle|M|,\circ^{M},\mathsf{I}^{M}\rangle
be a (finitely) presented monoid with finite generators
C=\{c_{1},\dots,c_{l}\}
such that the word problem for
M
is undecidable (by Markov
[
20
]
and Post
[
27
]
).
We define
S\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{c_{1}^{(1)},\dots,c_{l}^{(1)}\}\cup\{\mathsf{I}^{(0)}\}
and the
S
-algebra
A\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\langle|A|,\{f^{A}\}_{f^{(n)}\in S}\rangle
by:

|A|=|M|
;

c_{i}^{A}(x)=c_{i}\circ^{M}x
for
i\in[1,l]
;

\mathsf{I}^{A}=\mathsf{I}^{M}
.
By definition, for all two words
a_{1}\dots a_{n}
,
b_{1}\dots b_{m}
, over
C
:
they are equivalent in
M
iff

a_{1}(a_{2}(\dots a_{n}(\mathsf{I})\dots))\mathbin{\sim_{\{A\}}}b_{1}(b_{2}(\dots b_{m}(\mathsf{I})\dots))
.

In the rest of this section, we fix
\mathcal{C}
as a class of
S
-algebras.

2.1
On the finiteness of
k
-variable-occurrence fragment: from
1
to
k

How can we show the decidability of the equational theory of the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment?
We consider proving it from the finiteness up to the semantic equivalence relation:

Proposition 3

(Cor. of
[
6
,
18
]
for the complexity)
.

Let
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}
be a subterm-closed
3
3

3

A set
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}
is
subterm-closed
if for every
t\in T
, if
s
a subterm of
t
, then
s\in T
.
set.
If the set
T/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite,
the equational theory of
T
over
\mathcal{C}
is decidable.
Moreover, it is decidable in DLOGTIME-uniform
\mathrm{NC^{1}}
if the input is given as a well-bracketed string.

Proof Sketch.

Because
\mathcal{C}
is fixed and
T/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite, for each
t\in T
, one can calculate the index of the equivalence class of
t
on
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}
by using the (finite and possibly partial) Cayley table of each operator; thus, the equational theory is decidable.
Moreover, according to this algorithm, if the input is given as a well-bracketed string, one can also construct a parenthesis context-free grammar such that for all
t,s\in T
,
the well-bracketed string encoding the equation
t=s
is in the language iff
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}s
.
Hence, the complexity is shown because every language recognized by a parenthesis context-free grammar is in ALOGTIME
[
6
,
7
]
(ALOGTIME is equivalent to DLOGTIME-uniform
\mathrm{NC^{1}}

[
21
]
).
∎

For the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment, the finiteness of
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
(with
Proposition

3
) can imply the decidability of the equational theory of
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
(
Lemma

6
) by the following decomposition lemma.
Here, we write
t\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}[}s{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}/}a{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}]}
for the term
t
in which each
a
has been replaced with
s
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}
be a subterm-closed set.
Let
k\geq 2
,
a\in\Sigma
.
Then, for all
t\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}
, there are
t_{0}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)},f^{(n)}\in S,t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)}

such that
t=t_{0}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]
.

Proof.

By induction on
t
.
Since
k\geq 2
,
there are
g^{(m)}\in S,s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\in T
s.t.
t=g(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})

and
\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s_{i})=k
.
Case
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s_{i})\leq k-1
for all
i
:
By letting
t_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}a
, we have
t=t_{0}[g(s_{1},\dots,s_{m})/a]
.
Otherwise:
Let
i
be s.t.
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s_{i})=k
.
Since
s_{i}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}
,
let
{u}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)},f^{(n)}\in S,t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)}

be the ones obtained by IH w.r.t.
s_{i}
, so that
s_{i}={u}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]
.
By letting
t_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}g(s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1},{u},s_{i+1},\dots,s_{m})
,
we have:

\displaystyle t_{0}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]

\displaystyle=g(s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1},{u},s_{i+1},\dots,s_{m})[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]

\displaystyle=g(s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1},{u}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a],s_{i+1},\dots,s_{m})

(
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s_{j})=0
if
j\neq i
)

\displaystyle=g(s_{1},\dots,s_{i-1},s_{i},s_{i+1},\dots,s_{m})=t.

(
s_{i}={u}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]
)

Hence, this completes the proof.
∎

Example 5

(of
Lemma

4
)
.

If
S=\{\circ^{(2)},\mathsf{I}^{(0)}\}
and
a,b,c\in\Sigma
,
the term
t=\mathsf{I}\circ((a\circ(b\circ c))\circ\mathsf{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 3)}
has the following decomposition:

t=(\mathsf{I}\circ(a\circ\mathsf{I}))[a\circ(b\circ c)/a]
.
Then
\mathsf{I}\circ(a\circ\mathsf{I})\in\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
and
a,(b\circ c)\in\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 2)}
.
The following is an illustration of the decomposition, where the number written in each subterm
s
denotes
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s)
:

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.48264pt}{-19.47772pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\quad/\quad a\right].

Using this decomposition iteratively, we have the following:

Lemma 6
.

Let
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}
be a subterm-closed set.
Assume that
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.
Then, for each
k\in{\rm Nature}
, the set
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

It suffices to prove:
for all
k\geq 2
,
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.
By induction on
k
.
We have:

\displaystyle\mathop{\#}(T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}})

\displaystyle\leq\mathop{\#}(\{t_{0}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]\mid f^{(n)}\in S,t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)},t_{0}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}\}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}})

(
Lemma

4

)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{f^{(n)}\in S}\mathop{\#}(\{t_{0}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]\mid t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)},t_{0}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}\}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}).

Then the set
\{t_{0}[f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})/a]\mid t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)},t_{0}\in T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}\}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite
because
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k-1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite (by IH) and
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}
satisfies the congruence law.
Thus, the last term above is finite since
S
is finite.
Hence
T_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}=k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.
∎

2.2
The monoid of the
1
-variable-occurrence fragment

Thanks to
Lemma

6
, we can focus on the
1
-variable-occurrence fragment.
For the
1
-variable-occurrence fragment, it suffices to consider a monoid.
For a set
A
of characters, we write
A^{*}
for the set of all
words
(i.e., finite sequences) over the language
A
.
We write
wv
for the concatenation of words
w
and
v

and write
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\varepsilon}
for the empty word.
We write
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\|}w{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\|}
for the length of a word
w
.

Definition 7
.

Let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}
be the (possibly infinite) set of characters defined by:

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigcup_{f^{(n)}\in S,i\in[1,n]}\{f(t_{1},\dots,t_{i-1},\_,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})\mid\forall j\in[1,n]\setminus\{i\},\ t_{j}\in\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}\}.

(
\_
denotes “blank”.)
For a word
w\in\dot{\Sigma}^{*}
and a term
t\in\mathbf{T}
,
let
w\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}[}t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}]}
be the term defied by:

w\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}[}t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}]}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\begin{cases}f(t_{1},\dots,t_{i-1},v[t],t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})&(w=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{i-1},\_,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})v)\\
t&(w=\varepsilon)\end{cases}.

Example 8

(of
Definition

7
)
.

If
S=\{\circ^{(2)},\mathsf{I}^{(0)}\}
, then
\dot{\Sigma}=\{(\mathsf{I}\circ\_),((\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ\_),\dots(\_\circ\mathsf{I}),\dots\}
.
For example, if
w=((\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ\_)(\mathsf{I}\circ\_)(\_\circ\mathsf{I})
, we have:

\displaystyle w[a]=(((\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ\_)(\mathsf{I}\circ\_)(\_\circ\mathsf{I})[a])

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ((\mathsf{I}\circ\_)(\_\circ\mathsf{I})[a])

\displaystyle=(\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ(\mathsf{I}\circ((\_\circ\mathsf{I})[a]))=(\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ(\mathsf{I}\circ(a\circ\mathsf{I})).

Proposition 9
.

For all
t\in\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
, there are
w\in\dot{\Sigma}^{*}
and
s\in S^{(0)}\cup\Sigma
s.t.
t=w[s]
.

Proof.

By easy induction on
t
.
∎

Definition 10
.

Let
\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\sim}}}_{\mathcal{C}}
be the equivalence relation on
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}
defined by:

w\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\sim}}}_{\mathcal{C}}v\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\iff$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 5.0pt}}w[a]\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}v[a]\mbox{ where $a\in\Sigma$ is any variable.}

Lemma 11
.

If
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite, then
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

By
Proposition

9
(and that the set
S^{(0)}\cup\Sigma
is finite).
∎

Moreover, if
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}}
is finite, it suffices to consider a finite subset of
\dot{\Sigma}
, as follows:

Lemma 12
.

Assume that
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite.
Let
T_{0}=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}
be such that
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}=\{[t_{1}]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}},\dots,[t_{n}]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}\}
.
Let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}_{0}\subseteq\dot{\Sigma}
be the finite set defined by:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}_{0}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigcup_{f^{(n)}\in S,i\in[1,n]}\{f(t_{1},\dots,t_{i-1},\_,t_{i+1},\dots,t_{n})\mid\forall j\in[1,n]\setminus\{i\},\ t_{j}\in T_{0}\}.

Then
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite
\Longrightarrow

\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite.

Proof.

For every
a\in\dot{\Sigma}
, there is
b\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
s.t.
a\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}b
.
By the congruence law of
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
, for every
w\in\dot{\Sigma}^{*}
, there is some
v\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
s.t.
v\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}w
.
Since
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite, this completes the proof.
∎

Example 13

(of
Lemma

12
)
.

If
S=\{\circ^{(2)},\mathsf{I}^{(0)}\}
(so,
\dot{\Sigma}=\{(\mathsf{I}\circ\_),((\mathsf{I}\circ\mathsf{I})\circ\_),\dots(\_\circ\mathsf{I}),\dots\}
) and
\mathcal{C}
is the class of all monoids,
we have:
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}=\{[\mathsf{I}]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}\}
.
Thus the set
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}=\{(\mathsf{I}\circ\_),(\_\circ\mathsf{I})\}
is sufficient for considering the finiteness of
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Thus, to prove the finiteness of
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
,
it suffices to prove that both
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
are finite:

Lemma 14
.

If
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
are finite,
then for each
k\in{\rm Nature}
,
the set
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite (hence, the equational theory of
\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
over
\mathcal{C}
is decidable).

Proof.

We have:

\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
and
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
are finite

\Longrightarrow

\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite (by
Lemma

12
)

\Longrightarrow

\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite (by
Lemma

11
)

\Longrightarrow

\mathbf{T}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}}
is finite (by
Lemma

6
).
Hence by
Proposition

3
.
∎

2.3
Finiteness from finding equations

For the finiteness of
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
,
we consider finding equations
\{\langle w_{i},v_{i}\rangle\mid i\in I\}
and then applying the following:

Lemma 15
.

Let
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}\subseteq\dot{\Sigma}
be a finite set.
Let
(<)\subseteq(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})^{2}
be a well-founded relation s.t.

•

<
satisfies the congruence law (i.e.,
v<v^{\prime}\Longrightarrow wvw^{\prime}<wv^{\prime}w^{\prime}
);

•

<
has no infinite antichains.
4
4

4

This assumption is used only in the direction of
2
\Longrightarrow
1
.

Then, the following are equivalent:

1.

There is a finite set
\{\langle w_{i},v_{i}\rangle\mid i\in I\}\subseteq(<)\cap(\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}})
such that
the language
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
over the alphabet
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
is cofinite.
5
5

5

A language
L
over an alphabet
A
is
cofinite
if its complemented language
A^{*}\setminus L
is finite.

2.

\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite.

Proof.

1
\Longrightarrow
2
:
By induction on the well-founded relation
<
, we prove:
For every
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
, there is some
v\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})
such that

w\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}v
.
If
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})
, by letting
v=w
.
Otherwise, since
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
,
there are
i\in I
and
w^{\prime},w^{\prime\prime}\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
such that
w=w^{\prime}v_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
.
By
w^{\prime}w_{i}w^{\prime\prime}<w^{\prime}v_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
(the congruence law of
<
) and IH,
there is
v\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})
s.t.
w^{\prime}w_{i}w^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}v
.
We also have
w^{\prime}v_{i}w^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}w^{\prime}w_{i}w^{\prime\prime}
(by
v_{i}\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}w_{i}
with the congruence law of
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
).
Thus
w^{\prime}v_{i}w^{\prime\prime}\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}v
(by transitivity of
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
).

2
\Longrightarrow
1
:
Let
W\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\bigcup_{X\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}}\{w\in X\mid\mbox{$w$ is minimal w.r.t.\ $(<)\cap X^{2}$}\}
.
Let
\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
be the subword closure of
W
(i.e., the minimal set
W^{\prime}\supseteq W
s.t.
w^{\prime}ww^{\prime\prime}\in W^{\prime}\Longrightarrow w\in W^{\prime}
).
Let
V\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}(\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)\dot{\Sigma}_{0})\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
.
Then,
(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}V\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})=\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
holds, as follows.
For
\subseteq
:
Let
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
,
v\in V
,
w^{\prime}\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
.
If we assume
wvw^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
, then
v\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
, but this contradicts
v\in V
; thus,
wvw^{\prime}\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
.
For
\supseteq
:
By induction on the length of
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
.
If
w\in V
, clear.
Otherwise (i.e.,
w\not\in V
),
let
w=w^{\prime}a
(note that
w\neq\varepsilon
, because
\varepsilon\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
always by that
W
is not empty).
Then,
w^{\prime}\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)

(if not, since
w^{\prime}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
and
w\not\in\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
,
w\in V
, thus reaching a contradiction).
Thus by IH,
w^{\prime}\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}V\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
, and thus
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}V\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
.
Hence, we have
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}\setminus(\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}V\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*})=\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
.
Now, the set
W
is finite because
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite and for each
X\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
, the number of minimal elements
w
is finite (because
<
has no infinite antichains);
thus
\mathop{\mathrm{Subw}}(W)
is finite; thus
V
is finite.
Let
V=\{v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\}
.
For every
i\in[1,n]
, there is
w_{i}\in W\cap[v_{i}]_{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
s.t.
w_{i}<v_{i}
, because

v_{i}
is not minimal w.r.t.
(<)\cap[v_{i}]_{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}^{2}
.
Thus,
\{\langle w_{i},v_{i}\rangle\mid i\in[1,n]\}
is the desired set.
∎

The
shortlex order
(aka
length-lexicographical order
) is an example of
<
in
Lemma

15
(because it is a well-ordering
[
5
, Def. 2.2.3]

and its congruence raw is also easy).
While it is undecidable whether a given (finitely presented) monoid
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite
[
20
]
(see also
[
5
, Thm. 7.3.7 with Def. 7.3.2(b)]
) in general (cf.
2
of
Lemma

15
),
it is decidable (in linear time) whether the language of a given regular expression of the form
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
is cofinite (cf.
1
of
Lemma

15
):

Proposition 16
.

The following is decidable in linear time (more precisely,
\mathcal{O}(n)
time on a RAM machine for
n
the number of symbols in the given regular expression):
Given a regular expression of the form
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
over the alphabet
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
, is its language cofinite?

Proof Sketch.

By the Aho-Corasick algorithm,
we can construct a deterministic finite automaton (DFA) from a given regular expression of the form
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
in linear time
[
1
, Sect. 8]
.
By taking the complemented language of the DFA,
it suffices to show that the following problem is in
\mathcal{O}(n)
time: given a DFA with
n
states, is its language finite?
Then we can give the following algorithm:
From the graph induced by the DFA, remove all the states not reachable from the starting state and remove all the states not reachable to any accepting states by using the depth-first search;
check whether there exists some cycle in the graph by the depth-first search.
∎

Thus, thanks to
1
\Longrightarrow
2
of
Lemma

15
, we can focus on finding a finite set of equations.
While it is undecidable in general whether there exists such a set,
we give a possibly non-terminating pseudo-code in
Algorithm

1
,
which can help to find equations (e.g.,
Figure

1
).
6
6

6

Usually, to calculate
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is a bottleneck. For relaxing this problem, for example, hashing words by using some algebras in
\mathcal{C}
is practically useful for reducing the number of
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
calls (since if the hash of two words
w,v
are different, then we immediately have that
w,v
are not equivalent w.r.t.
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
).

Algorithm 1

Possibly non-terminating pseudo-code for ensuring the finiteness of
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
.

1:

Given
S,\Sigma
,
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
,
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
.
\triangleright

\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
is a finite alphabet of
Lemma

12
.

2:

Is
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
finite?

3:

\Gamma

\leftarrow

\emptyset

\triangleright
We use
I,w_{i},v_{i}
to denote
\Gamma=\{\langle w_{i},v_{i}\rangle\mid i\in I\}
.

4:

while
the language
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
over
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
is not cofinite
do

5:

\langle w,v\rangle

\leftarrow
a fresh pair in
X^{*}\times(X^{*}\setminus(X^{*}(\bigcup_{i\in I}v_{i})X^{*}))

s.t.
w<v

\triangleright

<
is a binary relation in (
Lemma

15
).

6:

if

w\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}v

then

\Gamma

\leftarrow

\Gamma\cup\{\langle w,v\rangle\}

7:

end

if

8:

end

while

9:

return

\mathsf{True}

Remark 17
.

When
\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}
is given as a total recursive function,
Algorithm

1
is a semi-algorithm (that is, if
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathcal{C}}}
is finite, the algorithm is terminated and returns
\mathsf{True}
; otherwise, not terminated).
This is because

\Longrightarrow

of

also holds.

3
The calculus of relations with bounded dot-dagger alternation

In the remaining part of this paper, as a case study of the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment presented in
Section

2
, we consider the calculus of relations with bounded dot-dagger alternation.
In this section, we recall the definitions of the calculus of relations (CoR) and the dot-dagger alternation hierarchy.

3.1
CoR: syntax and semantics

We fix
\Sigma
as a non-empty
finite
set of variables.
Consider the finite algebraic signature
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}S}^{\mathrm{CoR}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\bot^{(0)},\top^{(0)},-^{(1)},\cup^{(2)},\cap^{(2)},\mathsf{I}^{(0)},\mathsf{D}^{(0)},\cdot^{(2)},\dagger^{(2)}\}\cup\{\pi^{(1)}\mid\mbox{$\pi$ is a map in $[1,2]^{[1,2]}$}\}
(we consider algebras of binary relations and each
\pi
is used for a projection of binary relations).
The set
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
of
CoR terms
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}^{\mathrm{CoR}}\ni t,s,{u}\Coloneqq a

\displaystyle\mid\bot\mid\top\mid s\cup{u}\mid s\cap{u}\mid s^{-}

\displaystyle\mid\mathsf{I}\mid\mathsf{D}\mid s\cdot{u}\mid s\dagger{u}\mid s^{\pi}

(
a\in\Sigma
,
\pi\in[1,2]^{[1,2]}
).

Additionally, for a term
t
, we use
t^{\smile}
to denote the term
t^{\smile}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}t^{\{1\mapsto 2,2\mapsto 1\}}
.
Here, we use the infix notation for binary operators, the superscript notation for unary operators, and parenthesis in ambiguous situations, as usual.

For binary relations
R,S
on a set
W
,
the
identity relation

\mathsf{I}_{W}
on
W
, the
difference relation

\mathsf{D}_{W}
on
W
,
the
(relational) composition
(
relative product
)
R\cdot S
, the
dagger
(
relative sum
)
R\dagger S
, and the
projection

R^{\pi}
are defined by:

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}_{W}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x,y\rangle\in W^{2}\mid x=y\}

(identity)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{W}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x,y\rangle\in W^{2}\mid x\neq y\}

(difference)

\displaystyle R\cdot S

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x,y\rangle\in W^{2}\mid\exists z\in W,\ \langle x,z\rangle\in R\ \land\ \langle z,y\rangle\in S\}

(relative product)

\displaystyle R\dagger S

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x,y\rangle\in W^{2}\mid\forall z\in W,\ \langle x,z\rangle\in R\ \lor\ \langle z,y\rangle\in S\}

(relative sum)

\displaystyle R^{\pi}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\in W^{2}\mid\langle x_{\pi(1)},x_{\pi(2)}\rangle\in R\}

(projection).

A
structure

\mathfrak{A}
is a tuple
\langle|\mathfrak{A}|,\{a^{\mathfrak{A}}\}_{a\in\Sigma}\rangle
, where

|\mathfrak{A}|
is a non-empty set and
a^{\mathfrak{A}}\subseteq|\mathfrak{A}|^{2}
is a binary relation for each
a\in\Sigma
.
For a structure
\mathfrak{A}
,
the
binary relation
map
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\llbracket}\_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rrbracket}_{\mathfrak{A}}\colon\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}\to\mathop{\wp}(|\mathfrak{A}|^{2})
is the unique homomorphism extending
\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=a^{\mathfrak{A}}
w.r.t. the set-theoretic operators and the aforementioned binary relation operators; i.e.,

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\llbracket}t{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\rrbracket}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\llbracket a\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}a^{\mathfrak{A}}\hskip 5.0pt(a\in\Sigma)\hskip 15.00002pt\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\emptyset\hskip 15.00002pt\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}|\mathfrak{A}|^{2}\hskip 15.00002pt\llbracket\mathsf{I}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathsf{I}_{|\mathfrak{A}|}\qquad\llbracket\mathsf{D}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\mathsf{D}_{|\mathfrak{A}|}

\displaystyle\llbracket t\cup s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\cup\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\llbracket t\cap s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\cap\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\llbracket t^{-}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}|\mathfrak{A}|^{2}\setminus\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\llbracket t\cdot s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\cdot\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\llbracket t\dagger s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\dagger\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\llbracket t^{\pi}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}^{\pi}.

It is well-known that w.r.t. binary relations, CoR has the same expressive power as the three-variable fragment of first-order logic with equality:
7
7

7

Namely, for every formula
\varphi
with two distinct free variables
z_{1},z_{2}
in the three-variable fragment of first-order logic with equality, there is
t\in\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
such that for all
\mathfrak{A}
,
\llbracket\lambda z_{1}z_{2}.\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}
.
Conversely, for every
t\in\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
, there is
\varphi
such that for all
\mathfrak{A}
,
\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=\llbracket\lambda z_{1}z_{2}.\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}
.
Here,
\llbracket\lambda z_{1}z_{2}.\varphi\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{\langle x,y\rangle\in|\mathfrak{A}|^{2}\mid\mbox{$\varphi$ is true on $\mathfrak{A}$ if $z_{1},z_{2}$ are mapped to $x,y$, respectively}\}
.

Proposition 18

(
[
30
,
31
,
12
]
)
.

W.r.t. binary relations, the expressive power of
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
is equivalent to that of the three-variable fragment of first-order logic with equality.

Let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathsf{REL}}
be the class of all structures.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathsf{REL}}_{\geq m}
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathsf{REL}}_{\leq m}
) be the class of structures
\mathfrak{A}
of
\mathop{\#}|\mathfrak{A}|\geq m
(resp.
\mathop{\#}|\mathfrak{A}|\leq m
).
For
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathsf{REL}
, the equivalence relation
\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\sim}_{\mathcal{C}}}
on
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
is defined by:

t\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\sim}_{\mathcal{C}}}s\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\iff$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 5.0pt}}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=\llbracket s\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}
for every
\mathfrak{A}\in\mathcal{C}
.
For
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
, the
equational theory of
T
over
\mathcal{C}
is the set
\{\langle t,s\rangle\in T^{2}\mid t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathcal{C}}}s\}
.
We mainly consider
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}
: the equational theory over
\mathsf{REL}
.
The following are some instances w.r.t.
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}
:

\displaystyle a\cdot(b\cdot c)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(a\cdot b)\cdot c

\displaystyle a\cap(b\cup c)

\displaystyle\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(a\cap b)\cup(a\cap c)

\displaystyle(a^{\smile})^{\smile}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}a

\displaystyle a\cdot a^{\smile}

\displaystyle\mathbin{{\not\sim}_{\mathsf{REL}}}a^{\smile}\cdot a

\displaystyle a\cdot(b\dagger c)

\displaystyle\mathbin{{\not\sim}_{\mathsf{REL}}}(a\cdot b)\dagger c

\displaystyle a^{\{1\mapsto 1,2\mapsto 1\}}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(a\cap\mathsf{I})\cdot\top.

The following propositions hold because for each
m\in{\rm Nature}
, the number of structures
\mathfrak{A}
of
\mathop{\#}|\mathfrak{A}|\leq m
is finite up to isomorphism and each structure is finite.

Proposition 19
.

For each
m\in{\rm Nature}
,
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\leq m}}}}
is finite.

Proposition 20
.

Let
T\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
be a subterm-closed set and
m\geq 1
.
Then,
T/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite
\iff

T/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq m}}}}
is finite.
Additionally, the equational theory of
T
over
\mathsf{REL}
is decidable
\iff
the equational theory of
T
over
\mathsf{REL}_{\geq m}
is decidable.

Proof.

Because
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}s\iff t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\leq m-1}}}s\land t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq m}}}s
.
By
Proposition

19
with
Proposition

3
,
T/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\leq m-1}}}}
is finite and
the equational theory of
T
over
\mathsf{REL}_{\leq m-1}
is decidable.
∎

3.2
The dot-dagger alternation hierarchy

Definition 21

(the
dot-dagger alternation hierarchy

[
24
]
)
.

The sets,
\{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Sigma}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n},\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\Pi}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}\}_{n\in{\rm Nature}}
, are the minimal sets satisfying the following:

•

\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{0}=\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{0}=\{t\in\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}\mid\mbox{$t$ does not contain $\cdot$ nor $\dagger$}\}
;

•

For
n\geq 0
,
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}\cup\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}\subseteq\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n+1}\cap\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n+1}
;

•

For
n\geq 1
, if
s,{u}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
, then
s\cup{u},s\cap{u},s\cdot{u},s^{\pi}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
and
s\dagger{u}\in\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n+1}
;

•

For
n\geq 1
, if
s,{u}\in\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
, then
s\cup{u},s\cap{u},s\dagger{u},s^{\pi}\in\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
and
s\cdot{u}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n+1}
.

For example,
a\cdot b\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1}
and
a\cdot(b\dagger c)\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{2}
(the term
a\cdot b
means that for some
z
,
a(x,z)
and
b(z,y)
.
The term
a\cdot(b\dagger c)
means that for some
z
, for every
w
,
a(x,z)
and (
b(z,w)
or
c(w,y))
.
Here,
x
and
y
indicate the source and the target, respectively, and each
a^{\prime}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
denotes that there is an
a^{\prime}
-labelled edge from
x^{\prime}
to
y^{\prime}
).
The dot-dagger alternation hierarchy is an analogy of the quantifier alternation hierarchy in first-order logic (by viewing
\cdot
as
\exists
and
\dagger
as
\forall
).
This provides a fine-grained analogy of
Proposition

18
w.r.t. the number of quantifier alternations, as follows:

Proposition 22

(
[
24
, Cor. 3.14]
; cf.
Proposition

18
)
.

W.r.t. binary relations, the expressive power of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
(resp.
\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
) is equivalent to that of the level
\Sigma_{n}
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
) in the quantifier alternation hierarchy of the three-variable fragment of first-order logic with equality.

Because there are recursive translations for
Proposition

22

[
24
]
, the following (un-)decidability results follow from those in first-order logic.

Proposition 23
.

The equational theory of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
(resp.
\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
) is decidable if
n\leq 1

and is undecidable if
n\geq 2
.

Proof Sketch.

When
\Sigma
is a countably infinite set, they follow from the BSR class
[\exists^{*}\forall^{*},\mathrm{all}]_{=}

[
4
,
29
]
and the reduction class
[\forall\exists\land\forall^{3},(\omega,1)]

[
8
, Cor. 3.1.19]
.
We can strengthen this result even if
\mathop{\#}\Sigma=1
by using a variant of the translation in
[
22
, Lem. 11]
for encoding countably infinitely many variables by one variable.
(See
Appendix

A
for more details.)
∎

4
On the
k
-variable-occurence fragment of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}

We now consider
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
: the
k
-variable-occurrence fragment of the level
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
in the dot-dagger alternation hierarchy.
Clearly,
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}=\bigcup_{k\in{\rm Nature}}(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
.
While the equational theory of
\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n}
is undecidable in general (
Proposition

23
), we show that the equational theory of
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
is decidable (
Corollary

25
).
Our goal in this section is to show the following:

Theorem 24
.

For each
n,k\in{\rm Nature}
,
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Combining with
Proposition

3
yields the following decidability and complexity upper bound.
The complexity lower bound is because the equational theory can encode the
boolean sentence value problem

[
6
]
(even if
n=k=0
), as
a given boolean sentence
\varphi
is true iff
t\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}\top
, where
t
is the term obtained from
\varphi
by replacing
\land,\lor,\mathsf{T},\mathsf{F}
with
\cap,\cup,\top,\bot
, respectively.

Corollary 25
.

For
n,k\in{\rm Nature}
, the equational theory of
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
over
\mathsf{REL}
is decidable.
Moreover, it is complete for DLOGTIME-uniform
\mathrm{NC}^{1}
under DLOGTIME reductions if the input is given as a well-bracketed string.

To prove
Theorem

24
, we consider the finiteness of
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
in
Section

4.1

and the finiteness of a monoid for
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
in
Section

4.2
, respectively (cf.
Lemma

14
).

4.1
On the finiteness of
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}

For the finiteness of
\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
, by
Proposition

20
, it suffices to show the following:

Lemma 26
.

\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}}=\{[\bot]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}},[\top]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}},[\mathsf{I}]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}},[\mathsf{D}]_{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}}\}
.

Proof.

W.r.t.
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}
,
we prove that the four elements are closed under each operator.
For the operators
\cap,-,\cdot,\smile
, this is shown by the following Cayley tables:

\cap

\top

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\top

\top

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\bot

\bot

\bot

\bot

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{I}

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\bot

\mathsf{D}

\mathsf{D}

\bot

\bot

\mathsf{D}

-

\top

\bot

\bot

\top

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\mathsf{D}

\mathsf{I}

\cdot

\top

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\top

\top

\bot

\top

\top

\bot

\bot

\bot

\bot

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\top

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\mathsf{D}

\top

\bot

\mathsf{D}

\top

\smile

\top

\top

\bot

\bot

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{I}

\mathsf{D}

\mathsf{D}

Note that
\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\top
holds thanks to “
\geq 3
”.
When
\mathop{\#}(|\mathfrak{A}|)\geq 3
, we have:
\langle x,y\rangle\in\llbracket\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}

iff (
\exists z\in|\mathfrak{A}|,z\neq x\land z\neq y
) iff
|\mathfrak{A}|\setminus\{x,y\}\neq\emptyset
iff
\mathsf{True}
(cf.
Remark

27
).
(Similarly for
\mathsf{D}\cdot\top\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 2}}}\top
.)
For the other operators (
\cup,\dagger,\pi
), they can be expressed by using
\cap,-,\cdot,\smile
as follows:

t\cup s\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(t^{-}\cap s^{-})^{-}
,

t\dagger s\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(t^{-}\cdot s^{-})^{-}
,

t^{\{1\mapsto 1,2\mapsto 2\}}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}t
,

t^{\{1\mapsto 1,2\mapsto 1\}}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}(t\cap\mathsf{I})\cdot\top
,

t^{\{1\mapsto 2,2\mapsto 2\}}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}\top\cdot(t\cap\mathsf{I})
, and

t^{\{1\mapsto 2,2\mapsto 1\}}=t^{\smile}
.
Hence, this completes the proof.
∎

Remark 27
.

\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}\mathbin{{\not\sim}_{\mathsf{REL}}}\top
, whereas
\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\top
.
For example when
\mathop{\#}|\mathfrak{A}|=1
, since
\llbracket\mathsf{D}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=\llbracket\bot\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}
,
we have
\llbracket\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}=\emptyset\neq|\mathfrak{A}|=\llbracket\top\rrbracket_{\mathfrak{A}}
.
(
\mathsf{D}\cdot\mathsf{D}
is not equivalent to neither one of the four constants w.r.t.
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}
; thus, there are many constants w.r.t.
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}
.)

4.2
Monoid for
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}

Next, we decompose terms, and then we reduce the finiteness of
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
to that of a monoid (cf.
Section

2.2
).

Lemma 28
.

For each
n,k\in{\rm Nature}
,
if
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite,
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

By specializing
T
with
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
and
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathsf{REL}
, in
Proposition

3
and
Lemma

6
.
∎

Lemma 29
.

For each
n,k\in{\rm Nature}
,

(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite iff
(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

\Longleftarrow
:
For every term
t
in
(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
,
there is some
s
in
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}
such that
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}s^{-}
.
Such
s
can be obtained from the term
t^{-}
by taking the complement normal form using the following equations(see also
Section

B.1
):

\displaystyle\top^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}\bot

\displaystyle\bot^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}\top

\displaystyle\mathsf{I}^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}\mathsf{D}

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}\mathsf{I}

\displaystyle(s\cup{u})^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}s^{-}\cap{u}^{-}

\displaystyle(s\cap{u})^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}s^{-}\cup{u}^{-}

\displaystyle(s^{-})^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}s

\displaystyle(s^{\pi})^{-}

\displaystyle\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}(s^{-})^{\pi}.

\Longrightarrow
:
As with
\Longleftarrow
.
∎

Lemma 30

(cf.
Lemma

4
)
.

Let
a\in\Sigma
.
For all
n\geq 2
,
t\in(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
,
there are
t_{0}\in(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
and
t_{1}\in(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
such that
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}t_{0}[t_{1}/a]
.

Proof.

By induction on the pair of
n
and
t
.
We distinguish the following cases.
Case
t\in(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
:
Clear, by IH (
\because

\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-2}\subseteq\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1}
).
Case
t\in(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
:
By letting
t_{0}=a
and
t_{1}=t
.
Case
t=s\cup{u}
:
By
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(t)=1
,
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s)=0
or
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}({u})=0
holds.
Sub-case
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s)=0
:
By
Lemma

26
, let
s^{\prime}\in\{\bot,\top,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}
be s.t.
s\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}s^{\prime}
.
By IH w.r.t.
{u}
, let
{u}_{0}\in(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)},{u}_{1}\in(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
be s.t.
{u}\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}{u}_{0}[{u}_{1}/a]
.
By letting
t_{0}=s^{\prime}\cup{u}_{0}
and
t_{1}={u}_{1}
, we have
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}t_{0}[t_{1}/a]
.
Sub-case
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}({u})=0
:
As with Sub-case
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(s)=0
.
Case
t=s\cap{u},s\cdot{u},s^{\pi}
:
As with Case
t=s\cup{u}
.
∎

Lemma

30
:

Lemma 31
.

For each
n\in{\rm Nature}
, if
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite,
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

By induction on
n
.
Case
n\leq 1
:
By the assumption (note that
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{0})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}\subseteq(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
).
Case
n\geq 2
:
By the assumption,
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.
By IH with
Lemma

29
,
(\Pi^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n-1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.
Combining them with
Lemma

30
(and
Proposition

20
for changing
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}
and
\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}
mutually) yields that
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.
∎

For
S\subseteq S^{\mathrm{CoR}}
, let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\mathbf{T}}^{S}\subseteq\mathbf{T}^{\mathrm{CoR}}
be the set of all terms over the signature
S
.
Then we have:

Lemma 32
.

If
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite,
then
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Proof sketch.

Note that
t,s\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1}\Coloneqq{u}\mid t\cup s\mid t\cap s\mid t\cdot s\mid t^{\pi}
(where
{u}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{0}
,
\pi\in[1,2]^{[1,2]}
)
and
{u},{u}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{0}\Coloneqq a\mid{u}\cup{u}^{\prime}\mid{u}\cap{u}^{\prime}\mid{u}^{-}\mid\top\mid\bot\mid{u}^{\pi}
(where
a\in\Sigma
,
\pi\in[1,2]^{[1,2]}
).
By taking the complement (
-
) and projection (
\pi
) normal form and replacing
\bot
with
\mathsf{I}\cap\mathsf{D}
and
\top
with
\mathsf{I}\cup\mathsf{D}
, for each
t\in(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
and
a\in\Sigma
,
there are
t_{0}\in\mathbf{T}^{\{\cup,\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
and
t_{1}\in\mathbf{T}^{\{-\}\cup\{\pi\mid\pi\in[1,2]^{[1,2]}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
such that
t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}t_{0}[t_{1}/a]
.
Moreover, by the distributive law of
\cup
w.r.t.
\cdot
and
\cap
, for each
t\in\mathbf{T}^{\{\cup,\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
,
there are
n\in{\rm Nature}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}
such that

t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}t_{1}\cup\dots\cup t_{n}
.
Because
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite (by the assumption) and
\mathbf{T}^{\{-\}\cup\{\pi\mid\pi\in[1,2]^{[1,2]}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is clearly finite,
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{1})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.
Hence, this completes the proof.
(See
Appendix

B
for more details of the proof.)
∎

Combining
Lemmas

28
,
31
and
32
yields that to prove that
(\Sigma^{\mathrm{CoR}}_{n})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq k)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite,
it suffices to prove that
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}
be the set of characters of
Definition

7
from the signature
\{\cap^{(2)},\cdot^{(2)},\mathsf{I}^{(0)},\mathsf{D}^{(0)},\smile^{(1)}\}
.
That is,
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\Sigma}}\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 3.8889pt\hskip-3.8889pt\hbox{$=$}\hskip-3.8889pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 3.8889pt}}\{(\_\cap t),(t\cap\_),(\_\cdot t),(t\cdot\_)\mid t\in(\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D},\smile\}})_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 0)}\}\cup\{\smile\}
.
(While
\smile
does not occur in
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}
, we introduce
\smile
for replacing the primitive character
(\mathsf{D}\cdot\_)
with
\smile(\_\cdot\mathsf{D})
. This is not essential but is useful for reducing the number of equations and for simplifying the notation (
Definition

34
).)
Let
\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\dot{\sim}}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}
be the equivalence relation on
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}
defined by:

w\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}v\mathrel{\hbox{\hskip 5.0pt\hskip-5.0pt\hbox{$\iff$}\hskip-5.0pt\hskip-2.7431pt\raisebox{4.66875pt}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Delta$}}\hskip-2.7431pt\hskip 5.0pt}}w[a]\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}v[a]
where
a\in\Sigma
is any variable (recall
Definition

10
).
8
8

8

The condition “
\geq 5
” is needed for some equations in

1
(see also

).

Lemma 33
.

If
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}
is finite, then
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}}}}
is finite.

Proof.

Since
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}
is finite,
we have that
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D},\smile\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}}
is finite (
Lemma

11
);
thus,
\mathbf{T}^{\{\cap,\cdot,\mathsf{I},\mathsf{D}\}}_{(\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}\leq 1)}/{\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}}
is finite.
Hence by
Proposition

20
, this completes the proof.
∎

We consider the following finite subset
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
of
\dot{\Sigma}
(cf.
Lemma

12
):

Definition 34
.

Let
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}\subseteq\dot{\Sigma}
be the finite set
\{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\cap_{\mathsf{I}}},\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\cap_{\mathsf{D}}},\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\cdot_{\mathsf{D}}},\immediate{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.7}\smile}\}

,

are abbreviations of

,

,
respectively.

Lemma 35
.

If
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}
is finite, then
\dot{\Sigma}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}
is finite.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the following: for every
a\in\dot{\Sigma}
, there is
w\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}
such that
a\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}w
.
Case
a=(\_\cap t),(\_\cdot t)
:
Since
\mathop{\mathrm{vo}}(t)=0
, by using
Lemma

26
, they are shown by distinguishing the following four sub-cases, as follows:

t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\bot

t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\top

t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\mathsf{I}

t\mathbin{\sim_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 3}}}\mathsf{D}

a=(\_\cap t)

\cap_{\mathsf{I}}\cap_{\mathsf{D}}

\varepsilon

\cap_{\mathsf{I}}

\cap_{\mathsf{D}}

a=(\_\cdot t)

\cap_{\mathsf{I}}\cap_{\mathsf{D}}

\cdot_{\mathsf{D}}\cdot_{\mathsf{D}}

\varepsilon

\cdot_{\mathsf{D}}

Case
a=(t\cap\_),(t\cdot\_)
:
By
(t\cap\_)=\smile(\_\cap t)
and applying the above case analysis for
(-\cap t)
, this case can be proved (similarly for
(t\cdot\_)
).
Case
a=\smile
:
Since
\smile\in\dot{\Sigma}_{0}
.
∎

Thus, our goal is to prove that
\dot{\Sigma}_{0}^{*}/{\mathbin{\dot{\sim}}_{\mathsf{REL}_{\geq 5}}}
is finite.
